--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The current champion scores 0.00 with a single Stop state — robots don't move, so no items are delivered, and the score stays at 0. This is a trivially safe solution but not optimal. We need to push the score **below 0**, which means delivering items from the correct half (the one that decreases the score by 1).

**Key observations:**
- BLACK floor = source area (items available)
- WHITE floor = nest area (delivery)
- GRAY floor = transit zone
- BLUE walls behind one half of source, GREEN walls behind the other half
- One item type decreases score (-1), the other increases it (+1)
- We need robots to pick up only the correct item type and deliver it

**Strategy:**
- Robots should navigate to the source, preferably the correct half (approach BLUE or GREEN walls), then return to the nest (approach RED walls or move away from colored walls)
- We need to try both BLUE and GREEN sides to figure out which one decreases the score
- Use floor color to detect nest (WHITE) and source (BLACK)

**State designs:**

1. **Survival**: Keep the champion (Stop, score=0)

2. **2-state foraging**: Explore until BLACK floor → go toward nest (WHITE). This baseline foraging doesn't discriminate item type.

3. **2-state directed**: GoToColor(BLUE) until BLACK floor → GoToColor(RED/nest wall) until WHITE floor

4. **2-state directed**: GoToColor(GREEN) until BLACK floor → GoToColor(RED) until WHITE floor

5. **3-state with discrimination**: Explore → on BLACK go toward BLUE wall → on WHITE deliver (stop briefly)

6. **3-state**: Explore → GoToColor(BLUE) → GoToColor(RED) back to nest

7. **3-state**: Explore → GoToColor(GREEN) → GoToColor(RED) back to nest

8. **2-state**: GoToColor(BLUE) with floor-based return to nest

9. **2-state**: GoToColor(GREEN) with floor-based return

10-14: Variations mixing neighbor conditions and probabilities

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion scores -11.39 with a 2-state machine:
- State 0: GoToColor(GREEN, clr=3) → transitions to State 1 when floor is BLACK (p=0.9)
- State 1: GoToColor(RED, clr=1) → transitions to State 0 when floor is WHITE (p=0.9)

This means: go toward GREEN walls (to the GREEN half of the source), pick up item when on BLACK floor, then go toward RED wall (nest side), deliver when on WHITE floor. GREEN items decrease the score (good!).

**Exploitation strategy:**
- The core loop works: GoToColor(GREEN) → BLACK floor → GoToColor(RED) → WHITE floor → repeat
- Try tuning probabilities (0.9 → 0.7, 0.95, 1.0)
- Try adding more transitions to handle edge cases (e.g., avoid going to BLUE side)
- Try adding a 3rd state for better navigation
- Try GoAwayColor(BLUE) instead of GoToColor(GREEN) for the source phase

**Exploration:**
- 3-state machines with intermediate states
- Add neighbor-based conditions to help with congestion
- Try GoAwayColor(RED) to reach source area

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion (-12.16) is the same topology as before: GoToColor(GREEN) → BLACK floor → GoToColor(RED) → WHITE floor → repeat. The p=0.95 and p=1.0 variants didn't beat it, so p=0.9 seems near-optimal for this topology.

**Analysis:**
- The 2-state GREEN→RED machine is working well
- We need to explore whether we can do better with additional states or conditions
- Key ideas to try:
  1. Add a GoAwayColor(BLUE) state to actively avoid picking up wrong items
  2. Add neighbor-based repulsion to reduce congestion at the source
  3. Try slightly different probabilities around 0.9 (0.85, 0.88, 0.92)
  4. Add a 3rd state that helps navigate more efficiently (e.g., exploration when no color visible)
  5. Try having robots actively repel from BLUE walls while seeking GREEN
  6. Use GoAwayColor(RED) as an alternative way to approach the source

**Key insight:** The transition probability of 0.9 means robots occasionally stay in wrong state. Perhaps making transitions conditional on both floor color AND color perception could be more precise.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion remains at -12.16 after two rounds of exploitation. The 2-state GoToColor(GREEN)→GoToColor(RED) machine with p=0.9 is stable. None of the 3-state or 4-state variants have beaten it, and probability tuning hasn't helped either.

**Deep analysis:**
- The machine works by: approach GREEN walls (source) → detect BLACK floor → switch to approach RED wall (nest) → detect WHITE floor → repeat
- The score of -12.16 means ~12 successful deliveries per run on average
- Bottlenecks: (1) robots may crowd near source/nest, (2) some time wasted navigating across the gray zone, (3) no explicit avoidance of BLUE side

**New ideas to try:**
1. **Asymmetric probabilities**: Maybe p=0.9 for BLACK→switch is good but WHITE→switch could be different (try 0.7, 0.8)
2. **GoAwayColor(RED) for reaching source**: Instead of GoToColor(GREEN), try moving away from RED (nest wall) to reach source
3. **Add repulsion in transit**: A state that repels from neighbors when on gray floor (to reduce congestion)
4. **Camera-triggered transitions**: Switch to return state when perceiving RED (not just on WHITE floor)
5. **Multiple transition conditions**: Combine floor + camera perception for more reliable switching
6. **Different LED colors as signals**: Use LED colors to coordinate (though this is secondary)
7. **Try 4-state with explicit transit states**: GoToColor(GREEN) → GoAwayColor(BLUE) near source → GoToColor(RED) → Explore near nest

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 5 rounds, the champion is still -12.16 with the same 2-state machine. We've tried many variations without improvement. Let me think more carefully.

**Why is the champion stuck at -12.16?**
- 20 robots, 1200 steps, ~12 deliveries means roughly 1 delivery per robot per run... but only some robots are active foragers
- The machine is simple and effective but may have fundamental limitations:
  1. When on WHITE floor, robots switch back to GoToColor(GREEN) but may be already heading back into the source — the transition probability 0.9 means 10% of the time they stay in return-to-nest mode even when white
  2. Robots might cluster at source due to all going to same GREEN wall
  3. The gray zone transit is purely directional (GoToColor) which is good

**What hasn't been tried much:**
- Using GoAwayColor(BLUE) to push toward GREEN side (complementary to GoToColor(GREEN))
- Using GoAwayColor(RED) to reach source from nest side
- Using color perception (C=7) as the PRIMARY transition trigger (when robot sees RED wall, switch to return)
- Having robots use neighbor count to spread out
- 3-state with explicit exploration in gray zone

**New hypothesis:** Maybe the bottleneck is in the gray zone. Adding an intermediate exploration or attraction state could help spread robots. Also, let's try camera-based transitions (perceive RED → start returning) which could be faster than waiting for WHITE floor.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Six rounds in and the champion is still -12.16. The 2-state GREEN→RED machine is very robust but we can't seem to beat it. Let me think about what fundamentally limits it.

**Fundamental analysis:**
- 20 robots, 1200 steps = 120 seconds
- A round trip (source to nest and back) in a typical arena takes maybe 15-20 seconds
- So theoretically 6-8 trips per robot × 20 robots = 120-160 possible deliveries, but we only get ~12
- This means only ~1-2 robots are actually completing the loop efficiently, OR robots are spending too much time not going the right direction

**Key insight I may have missed:** The transition from GoToColor(GREEN) to GoToColor(RED) happens when the robot is on BLACK floor (p=0.9). But what if the robot is already heading back (in GoToColor(RED) state) and happens to cross BLACK floor again? It would stay in the RED state (since the transition is FROM state 0). So the loop seems correct.

**Why only ~12 deliveries?** Perhaps many robots get stuck near the GREEN wall or the RED wall. Or the color perception at distance causes robots to oscillate.

**New ideas:**
1. Try `clr=4` (yellow) or `clr=5` (magenta) or `clr=6` (cyan) - wait, those are LED colors not wall colors. Wall colors are RED=1, GREEN=3 (or is it 2?), BLUE=2 (or 3?). Let me reconsider color mapping.

Actually the color IDs are: {1,2,3,4,5,6}. The champion uses clr=3 for GREEN walls and clr=1 for RED walls. Let me try clr=2 for BLUE and see if BLUE side is actually better than GREEN side.

**Critical test:** Try GoToColor(BLUE/clr=2) instead of GREEN to see if BLUE gives better (more negative) score. Also try different color combos.

Also try: what if we use GoToColor(GREEN) but add a GoAwayColor(BLUE) to keep robots away from the BLUE half?

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

We have a new champion at -12.78! It's a 3-state machine:
- State 0: GoToColor(GREEN/clr=3) → when BLACK floor (p=0.9) → go to State 1
- State 1: GoToColor(RED/clr=1) → when WHITE floor (p=0.9) → go to State 1 (wait, n1x0 destination is 1... with 3 states, leaving state 1, others are [0,2], so D=1 means state 2)
- State 2: GoAwayColor(BLUE/clr=2) → when BLACK floor (p=0.9) → go to State 1; OR when perceiving GREEN (l=3, p=0.9) → go to State 0

So the logic is:
- State 0: Seek GREEN wall → on BLACK floor → switch to return (State 1)
- State 1: GoToColor(RED) → on WHITE floor → switch to State 2 (GoAwayColor(BLUE))
- State 2: GoAwayColor(BLUE) → on BLACK floor → return (State 1); OR perceive GREEN → go to State 0

State 2 acts as a "corrections" state that actively avoids BLUE while heading back or repositioning. When it sees GREEN, it goes to the GoToColor(GREEN) state.

**Exploitation ideas:**
1. Tune probabilities (0.9 → 0.85, 0.95)
2. Change State 2 transitions slightly
3. Add more transitions to existing states
4. Try different destination indices

**Key insight:** State 2 (GoAwayColor(BLUE)) after nest delivery helps robots naturally drift toward the GREEN side. This is clever because moving away from BLUE pushes toward GREEN.

Let me also try:
- Having State 2 transition to State 0 on BLACK floor (instead of State 1)
- Adding a 4th state
- Changing the GoAwayColor target in State 2

## Reasoning

Excellent! A big jump to -19.44! The new champion is a 3-state machine:
- **State 0**: GoToColor(GREEN/clr=3) → on BLACK floor (p=0.9) → go to State 1
- **State 1**: GoToColor(RED/clr=1) → on WHITE floor (p=0.9) → go to State 2 (dest D=1 from state 1 means state 2); OR perceive BLUE (clr=2, p=0.8) → go to State 2
- **State 2**: GoAwayColor(BLUE/clr=2) → on BLACK floor (p=0.9) → go to State 1; OR perceive GREEN (clr=3, p=0.9) → go to State 0

The key improvement: State 1 (returning to nest) now ALSO triggers when it perceives BLUE walls. This means when carrying a GREEN item and heading back, if it accidentally drifts toward BLUE side, it quickly switches to GoAwayColor(BLUE) state. Very clever!

**Exploitation:**
1. Tune probabilities around the champion values (0.9/0.8 → 0.85/0.75, 0.95/0.85)
2. Add a 3rd transition to some states
3. Try adding floor-based transitions to State 2
4. Try WHITE floor trigger for State 2→State 0 directly

**Exploration:**
1. 4-state extension of this topology
2. Add gray floor detection
3. Try different GoAwayColor targets

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Final round! Champion is -19.44. The 3-state machine has been stable. Let me analyze it carefully:

- **State 0**: GoToColor(GREEN/clr=3) → BLACK floor (p=0.9) → State 1
- **State 1**: GoToColor(RED/clr=1) → WHITE floor (p=0.9) → State 2; OR perceive BLUE (p=0.8) → State 2
- **State 2**: GoAwayColor(BLUE/clr=2) → BLACK floor (p=0.9) → State 1; OR perceive GREEN (p=0.9) → State 0

The logic: seek GREEN source → pick up → return to RED nest → deliver → GoAwayColor(BLUE) to reposition toward GREEN side → repeat.

State 1 has a clever dual trigger: white floor OR perceiving BLUE (means drifted to wrong side while returning). State 2 repositions away from BLUE and when it sees GREEN, goes to seek it.

**What's been tried and didn't work:** p=0.95, p=0.85, extra transitions. The topology seems near-optimal.

**Final round strategies:**
1. Keep exact champion
2. Fine-tune probabilities very precisely (0.88, 0.92, 0.87, 0.93)
3. Try adding a 4th state as an explicit "pick up from GREEN" intermediary
4. Try slight variations on transition destinations
5. Try adding perception of RED in State 0 to avoid nest area
6. Try GoAwayColor(RED) as state 2 instead of GoAwayColor(BLUE) - different repositioning
7. Try p=1.0 for some transitions (deterministic)
8. Add gray floor detection to prevent robots getting confused

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
